=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums.
Any positive divisor of a natural number x that is strictly less than x is called a proper divisor of x. For example, 2 is a proper divisor of 4, while 6 is not a proper divisor of 6.
You are allowed to perform an operation any number of times on nums, where in each operation you select any one element from nums and divide it by its greatest proper divisor.
Return the minimum number of operations required to make the array non-decreasing.
If it is not possible to make the array non-decreasing using any number of operations, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [25,7]
Output: 1
Explanation:
Using a single operation, 25 gets divided by 5 and nums becomes [5, 7].

Example 2:

Input: nums = [7,7,6]
Output: -1

Example 3:

Input: nums = [1,1,1,1]
Output: 0

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

For non-decreasing, if process from right to left, we need b_i <= b_{i+1}. To minimize operations, maybe choose b_i as large as possible (<= b_{i+1})? Is this greedy optimal? Let's test. We process right to left, fixing b_{i+1} as chosen. For current, choosing the largest possible value <= b_{i+1} minimizes operations (prefer original) and maximizes current value, which is best for previous elements (they need <= current). If original not allowed, only reduced. So current choice is forced by threshold b_{i+1}: if original <= threshold, choose original cost0; else if reduced <= threshold, choose reduced cost1; else impossible. There is no alternative with same cost. Thus greedy from right to left is optimal and simpler. Let's verify with examples.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)
        spf = [0]*(max_val+1)
        # compute smallest prime factor
        if max_val >= 1:
            spf[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, max_val+1):
            if spf[i] == 0:
                spf[i] = i
                if i * i <= max_val:
                    for j in range(i*i, max_val+1, i):
                        if spf[j] == 0:
                            spf[j] = i
        # But this sieve has issue: for primes > sqrt(max), spf set to i. For composite, spf set by smaller prime. Need ensure all composites get spf. Standard:
        # for i in range(2, N+1):
        #   if spf[i]==0:
        #       spf[i]=i
        #       for j in range(i*i, N+1, i):
        #           if spf[j]==0: spf[j]=i
        # This works because when i is prime, mark multiples starting i*i. But what about composite j that has smallest prime factor p but j < p*p? impossible? If p is smallest prime factor, j >= p*p? Not necessarily if j = p * q with q > p, then j >= p*(p+1) > p*p? Actually q >= p, so j >= p^2. Yes. So starting at i*i okay. But if composite has smallest prime factor p, it will be marked when i=p at j=p*p? Wait j=p*q, q>=p, so j>=p^2, so in range. Good. But if p^2 > max? Then j cannot have smallest factor p? If p^2 > max and j multiple of p, then j = p * q with q>=p, so j>=p^2>max, impossible. So no composite with smallest factor p. So okay.
        # However inner loop `if spf[j]==0` marks only first time. Since i loops increasing, first time is smallest prime. Good.
        # Need set spf[1]? Not used for x=1. Could set 1.

But this sieve is O(N log log N) but with Python nested loops and if. For N=1e6, okay. But let's examine performance: Outer loop 1e6, inner for primes up to 1000. Total ~? Sum N/p for p<=1000 ~ N log log 1000 ~ maybe 2.8e6? Actually primes up to 1000, sum 1/p ~ log log 1000 + M ~2.0, so ~2e6 iterations. Outer 1e6 with if. Fine.

But there is a subtle bug: In the loop, for i composite, spf[i] already set, skip. For i prime, set spf[i]=i and mark multiples. For i=2, mark 4,6,8,...; spf[4]=2. For i=3, mark 9,12,15,...; spf[12] already 2, skip. Good.

Now greedy:

def minOperations(self, nums):
    if not nums: return 0? Constraints length >=1.
    max_val = max(nums)
    spf = [0]*(max_val+1)
    if max_val >= 1:
        spf[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, max_val+1):
        if spf[i] == 0:
            spf[i] = i
            if i * i <= max_val:
                step = i
                start = i*i
                for j in range(start, max_val+1, step):
                    if spf[j] == 0:
                        spf[j] = i
    ans = 0
    # process from right to left
    # We need maintain next value (b_{i+1}) as chosen for suffix.
    # Start with last element original? Let's implement loop from n-1 down to 0 with next_val = None? Actually for i = n-1, no right constraint, choose original cost0. Then for i < n-1, next_val = chosen value of i+1.
    # Simpler:
    n = len(nums)
    # set last chosen to nums[-1] (original)
    next_val = nums[-1]
    # For i from n-2 down to 0:
    for i in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        x = nums[i]
        if x <= next_val:
            # keep x, cost 0; next_val = x
            # But wait if x=1, okay. If x >1 prime, x<=next_val keep. 
            next_val = x
        else:
            # x > next_val, need reduce if possible
            if x == 1:
                return -1 # 1 cannot reduce and > next_val impossible (next_val>=1)
            p = spf[x]
            if p <= next_val:
                ans += 1
                next_val = p
            else:
                return -1
    return ans

But is this correct for last element? We set next_val = nums[-1] without considering that last could be reduced to help previous? As argued no, because reducing last makes it smaller, making constraint for previous harder and costs. But wait, if last is composite and original large, previous might be impossible to be <= original, but possible <= spf(last). Reducing last would make previous easier? No, previous needs <= last. If last is smaller, previous must be even smaller, so harder. Example nums [100, 6]. Last 6 original. Previous 100 cannot <=6; reduce 100 ->2 <=6, possible. If we reduced last to 2, previous 100 cannot <=2, impossible. So original last is best. If previous is small, original last okay. Thus yes.

But consider if last original is prime? same.

What about choosing last original cost0 but if previous cannot be <= original but could be <= reduced? Wait if previous value > original, reducing last to smaller won't help because previous > smaller still. If previous value <= reduced, then it's also <= original (since reduced < original). So original last never hurts. Good.

Now, is right-to-left greedy with "if x <= next_val keep x" always optimal? Suppose x <= next_val, keeping x (largest) cost0. Could reducing x (cost1, smaller) ever help earlier elements? Earlier elements need <= x. Smaller x makes constraint harder, so no. It costs. Thus keep.

If x > next_val, need reduce to p=spf[x]. But what if x is prime, p=x > next_val, impossible. If composite, p < x. If p <= next_val, reduce. Could there be a way to instead reduce next_val (i+1) to make it larger? No, operation only decreases, so next_val cannot be increased. We already chose next_val as large as possible for suffix. If next_val were larger, current might not need reduce; but greedy chose next_val as large as possible. Need ensure greedy's choice of next_val is indeed maximum feasible for suffix, because if we had chosen a larger next_val (by not reducing some later element), current could avoid reduction and total cost lower. Wait! This is important. Right-to-left greedy chooses for each element the largest possible value <= next_val. Starting from last original. For i+1, greedy chose largest possible value given i+2. This maximizes b_{i+1}. Thus if current x > b_{i+1}, any feasible suffix must have b_{i+1} <= that maximum? Actually b_{i+1} could be smaller, not larger. Since greedy chose maximum, if x > maximum, then x > any feasible b_{i+1}, so current cannot keep. If x <= maximum, greedy keeps. Good. So greedy's next_val is maximum feasible value for suffix. Need prove by induction.

Let's test with a case where reducing a later element could allow current to avoid reduction? Reducing later makes it smaller, so current would need be <= smaller, harder, not avoid. So no. What about not reducing a later element when greedy did reduce? Greedy only reduces later if original > next_next and reduced <= next_next. If it didn't reduce, original would violate non-decreasing with next_next. So forced. Thus greedy maximizes.

spf=[0]*(N+1); primes=[]

        v=p*i

        spf[v]=p
```

Now, let's search for counterexamples to greedy. We'll brute force mentally or maybe reason. Since each element choices at most 2, right-to-left greedy is known for "make array non-decreasing by optionally replacing each element with smaller alternative" where alternative is smaller and costs 1. Let's prove with exchange argument.

Let choices for each i: high H_i = nums[i], low L_i = spf(nums[i]) if nums[i]>1 else H_i. L_i <= H_i. Cost 0 for H, 1 for L (if L<H; if equal, H dominates).

We want min cost non-decreasing sequence b_i ∈ {L_i,H_i}. Greedy from right:
- Let b_n = H_n (cost0).
- For i=n-1..1, choose largest feasible value <= b_{i+1}; if none impossible.

Induction: After processing suffix i+1..n, greedy has chosen a sequence with minimum cost among all feasible suffixes and with b_{i+1} as large as possible? Need stronger: among all feasible suffixes for positions i+1..n, greedy's b_{i+1} is maximum possible, and its cost is minimal? Let's examine. Greedy from right to left chooses largest feasible at each step. Does it also minimize cost for suffix? Since it prefers H (cost0) when feasible, and L only when H infeasible. So cost is minimal for the chosen suffix? But could a different suffix with smaller b_{i+1} (cost maybe lower?) allow lower total cost? Wait cost is per element; choosing L costs 1, H costs0. Greedy chooses H whenever possible, so it minimizes cost locally. But could choosing L at position i+1 (cost1) allow position i+2 to choose H instead of L, reducing total cost? Since processing right-to-left, position i+2 is already fixed. Choosing L at i+1 makes b_{i+1} smaller, which makes constraint for i+2? Actually i+2 is to the right; non-decreasing requires b_{i+1} <= b_{i+2}. If b_{i+1} is smaller, it's easier to satisfy b_{i+1} <= b_{i+2}, but b_{i+2} is fixed. Wait! This is crucial: When processing right-to-left, we are choosing b_{i+1} given b_{i+2}. If b_{i+1} is smaller, it is easier to satisfy b_{i+1} <= b_{i+2} (right constraint), but harder for left elements b_i <= b_{i+1}. Greedy chooses largest feasible b_{i+1} <= b_{i+2}. Choosing largest makes right constraint satisfied (since <= b_{i+2}) and left constraint easiest. It also costs less (H if feasible). So no tradeoff. But could choosing smaller b_{i+1} (cost1) allow b_{i+2} to be smaller? No, b_{i+2} is already chosen before; if we later decide to change b_{i+2}, that's not considered. But greedy's b_{i+2} is largest feasible for its right. If we choose smaller b_{i+1}, it doesn't affect feasibility of b_{i+2} (b_{i+1} <= b_{i+2} remains true if smaller). It only adds cost and hurts left. So not beneficial. Thus induction holds.

Let's think of formal invariant:
At step i, `next_val` is the largest possible value for position i+1 among all feasible non-decreasing completions of suffix i+1..n, and `ans` is the minimum operations for that suffix (with that largest value). Actually if there are multiple feasible suffixes with different b_{i+1}, the one with largest b_{i+1} may have more operations? Wait largest b_{i+1} is achieved by choosing H if feasible, which costs 0 for that element, but might force earlier? Earlier not processed yet. Thus greedy's b_{i+1} is both largest and minimum cost for suffix? Let's test if choosing H at i+1 could force i+2? No, i+2 already fixed; H <= b_{i+2} ensures feasibility. So yes. If H > b_{i+2}, L forced; cost1. So greedy suffix is optimal.

Now, code style:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)
        spf = [0] * (max_val + 1)
        if max_val >= 1:
            spf[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, max_val + 1):
            if spf[i] == 0:
                spf[i] = i
                if i * i <= max_val:
                    for j in range(i * i, max_val + 1, i):
                        if spf[j] == 0:
                            spf[j] = i

        n = len(nums)
        ans = 0
        nxt = nums[-1]
        for i in range(n - 2, -1, -1):
            x = nums[i]
            if x <= nxt:
                nxt = x
            else:
                p = spf[x]
                if p <= nxt:
                    ans += 1
                    nxt = p
                else:
                    return -1
        return ans
```

```

Define H_i = nums[i], L_i = spf(nums[i]) if nums[i]>1 else H_i. L_i <= H_i. Cost c(H_i)=0, c(L_i)=1 if L_i<H_i else 0 (but if equal, no need). We want min cost non-decreasing b.

Lemma: For any suffix starting at i, there exists an optimal solution in which b_i is as large as possible among all feasible b_i values given b_{i+1}? Actually greedy chooses maximum feasible. We can prove by backward induction:
Base: b_n = H_n is maximum and min cost for last element.
Inductive step: Suppose for suffix i+1..n, greedy has chosen b_{i+1} = M, the maximum possible value for position i+1 among all feasible completions of suffix i+1..n, and with minimum cost for that suffix. For position i, to be feasible with this suffix, need b_i <= M. The largest feasible value for b_i is H_i if H_i <= M, else L_i if L_i <= M, else impossible. Greedy chooses that. Any solution with a different b_i cannot have a larger b_i (by definition), and if it chooses a smaller value, it cannot reduce cost below greedy (if H_i feasible, greedy cost0; if only L_i feasible, greedy cost1; smaller than L_i impossible). Also a smaller b_i would only make constraints on prefix harder. Thus greedy choice is optimal and maintains maximum for position i. Need also consider if a different suffix with smaller b_{i+1} could allow a smaller b_i? We don't want smaller. If greedy's b_{i+1}=M is maximum, then if H_i <= M, greedy keeps H_i. If H_i > M, H_i > any other b_{i+1}, so H_i infeasible with any suffix. If L_i <= M, greedy reduces. If L_i > M, L_i > any other b_{i+1}, so impossible. Thus suffix choice doesn't matter except M. Greedy's M is maximum, so it gives the best chance. Good.

Now, let's think about if choosing H_i when H_i <= M could somehow force a different b_{i+1} to be smaller? No, b_{i+1} already fixed. H_i <= M ensures feasibility. Good.

Thus correct.

Previous element y=3 (prime). Then previous 3 <= 4, feasible. If instead we reduced current 4 to 2, previous 3 <= 2 would be false, so keeping original is better. Good. If previous y=5, 5<=4 false; reducing current to2 also false. So keeping original dominates. Good.

Could there be an alternative where we reduce position i+2 to make nxt original larger? No, nxt original is fixed value of nums[i+1] or its spf; cannot increase. Greedy chose nxt as large as possible. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "nxt" if we process from right to left and choose original for last. Suppose last is composite and original > previous, but previous can be reduced to p <= original. Greedy works. Suppose last is composite and original < previous, but previous can be reduced
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)
        spf = [0] * (max_val + 1)
        if max_val >= 1:
            spf[1] = 1

        for i in range(2, max_val + 1):
            if spf[i] == 0:
                spf[i] = i
                if i * i <= max_val:
                    for j in range(i * i, max_val + 1, i):
                        if spf[j] == 0:
                            spf[j] = i

        ans = 0
        nxt = nums[-1]

        for i in range(len(nums) - 2, -1, -1):
            x = nums[i]
            if x <= nxt:
                nxt = x
            else:
                p = spf[x]
                if p <= nxt:
                    ans += 1
                    nxt = p
                else:
                    return -1

        return ans
```
